By February 2021, about 45,000 civilians had been forced to leave the zone due to extrajudicial killings, arbitrary detentions, and the disappearances of people, especially young men.[^cite_note-ReliefWeb-2021-380] One refugee interviewed by Reuters said that if they didn't leave when they did, they would have been killed by Amhara forces. 41 other refugees interviewed have also described attacks, looting, and threats by Amhara forces.[^cite_note-Reuters-2021-70]

In March 2021, [Mulu Nega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulu_Nega), then leader of the federal-government-appointed Transitional Government of Tigray,[^cite_note-382] stated that Amhara *de facto* administrators of Western Tigray used violence against ethnic Tigrayans and forcibly displaced them.[^cite_note-Reuters-2021-70] Yabsira Eshetie, the administrator of the area, denied the claims. The [Amhara government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Amhara_Region) also denied the reports of forced displacement and asked the Ethiopian government to modify the border between the Amhara and Tigray regions. US Secretary of State Antony Blinken stated that there had been acts of ethnic cleansing in Tigray.[^cite_note-Reuters-2021-70]

#### Refugees in Sudan

Further information: [Ethiopian refugees in Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_refugees_in_Sudan)



Ethiopian refugee camp in Sudan

Some Tigrayans who fled to Sudan as refugees would later find themselves victimized again by [human traffickers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_traffickers) upon arrival. Many refugees had testified being kidnapped, regularly tortured, and transported to warehouses in [Libya](17633.md), where many would die due to poor living conditions.[^cite_note-Harter-2023-383] Eritrean refugees faced similar risks, as, in addition to the traffickers, they also risked getting kidnapped by the Eritrean government.[^cite_note-384] This situation [worsened](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Sudanese_refugee_crisis) further after Sudan [descended into violence in 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Sudan_\(2023\)), placing refugees at further risk of being kidnapped.[^cite_note-Harter-2023-383][^cite_note-385]

#### Eritrean refugees

Further information: [Refoulement of Eritrean refugees during the Tigray war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refoulement_of_Eritrean_refugees_during_the_Tigray_war)

In November 2020, the UN warned of "very critical" supply shortages for the nearly 100,000 Eritrean refugees who, prior to the war, were registered in four camps in Tigray region.[^cite_note-france24_ref_5-374][^cite_note-386] Later that same month, the UN reported that people in Tigray were fleeing Mekelle. The federal government had warned of "no mercy" if Tigray forces and residents remained intermingled.[^cite_note-france24_ref_5-374][^cite_note-euronews_ref_2-387] As of 2 February 2021, 20,000 of the Eritrean refugees in Tigray, mostly from the [Hitsats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitsats_massacre) and Shimelba camps, remained unaccounted for, according to the [United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees](31964.md).[^cite_note-388]

## Internet and media

Internationally the Tigray war received less coverage compared to other conflicts taking place at the time.[^cite_note-389]

### Role of online social networks

Main article: [Mass media in Ethiopia](5967520.md)

Claire Wilmot, writing in *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, found that a significant number of new, single-issue [Twitter](9988187.md) accounts were opened in the immediate aftermath of the Northern Command attacks. Most appeared to be authentic accounts from people seeking to raise international awareness of the conflict in the midst of a communications blackout in Tigray.[^cite_note-WashPost_Tigrayconflict_social_networks-390] The Ethiopian government cited disinformation and hate speech to justify communications blackouts. Researchers suggested that reducing access to information could help to create contexts where misinformation can thrive because it reduces the ability to verify information.[^cite_note-WashPost_Tigrayconflict_social_networks-390][^cite_note-WashPost_whats_going_on-391] In late July 2021, a report emerged that there was coordination in social media messaging and media reportage of the conflict in Ethiopia, to an extent it mirrored an earlier Syrian hybrid information campaign designated as Project Basma.[^cite_note-Geopolitics_Press-392]

The Ethiopian government tried to control the information environment by positioning itself as the sole provider of reliable information.[^cite_note-WashPost_whats_going_on-391] In February, pro-government groups called on their supporters in Ethiopia and the diaspora to combat what they called "TPLF fake news" online.[^cite_note-393] Pro-government groups used tactics similar to those of pro-Tigray groups to push their narrative of the conflict, though as of 5 February, pro-government campaigns had produced fewer Tweets overall.[^cite_note-WashPost_whats_going_on-391] Both *[Agence France-Presse](471981.md)* and *[BBC News](1139893.md)* have documented examples of old or manipulated photos, which misleadingly endorsed either the federal Ethiopian government or the TPLF.[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395]

Researchers found that groups use tactics such as "copy and paste" campaigns hosted on websites, which include instructions for opening new accounts, copying and pasting pre-written tweets, and tagging [influencers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_celebrity). Both campaigns produced disinformation and misinformation, though the majority of content produced was activist in nature.[^cite_note-WashPost_whats_going_on-391] Wilmot suggested that the lines between authentic political activity and deliberate manipulation of online content during the conflict were increasingly blurred.[^cite_note-WashPost_Tigrayconflict_social_networks-390]

#### Facebook

See also: [Facebook content management controversies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook_content_management_controversies)

[Facebook](7529378.md) has been heavily criticized for its perceived role in fuelling ethnic tensions during the war, and has faced accusations that, in choosing not to crack down on [hate speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_speech) being spread by Ethiopian users, it is complicit in cases of ethnic cleansing in the country.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399][^cite_note-400]

### Restriction of media coverage

Further information: [Democratic backsliding in Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_Ethiopia) and [Internet in Ethiopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_Ethiopia)

The Ethiopian government had engaged in repeated crackdowns on media coverage throughout the war.[^cite_note-401] In November 2021 (during the [2021–2022 state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_Ethiopian_state_of_emergency)), the Ethiopian Media Authority (EMA) threatened to cancel the media licences of *BBC News*, *Reuters*, *[CNN](62028.md)* and *Associated Press*, accusing the news organisations of having "consistently disseminated news that sowed seeds of animosity among people and compromised the sovereignty of the country." The EMA cited "reporting the Law enforcement operation as a [genocidal campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_crimes_in_the_Tigray_war#Genocide_claims)" as an example of misleading information aiming to "undermin\[e] the \[federal] government's efforts to address the humanitarian crisis in the Tigray region."[^cite_note-402]

On 20 May 2022, Ethiopian law enforcement began arresting journalists en masse, with 4,500 people in the Amhara Region alone being taken into custody. One Amhara law enforcement official described this as a way to maintain "[law and order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_order_\(politics\))" and "get rid of outside enemies".[^cite_note-403]

The media restrictions resulted in what has been described as an "[information blackout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_blackout)".[^cite_note-404] Many journalists, both local and international, have noted the difficulty they face in trying to report on the war, as they risk the possibility of getting either killed or imprisoned by government forces.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406] By December 2021, the *[Committee to Protect Journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_to_Protect_Journalists)* described both Ethiopia and Eritrea as the worst "jailers of journalists" in [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md).[^cite_note-407]

## Peace process

Main article: [Tigrayan peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigrayan_peace_process)

Several proposals for [peace negotiations and mediation](24903.md) were made involving some of the main groups involved in the war. Of these, this includes: an emergency [Intergovernmental Authority on Development](1921406.md) summit in December 2020;[^cite_note-IGAD_38_communique-408] a joint statement by the [National Congress of Great Tigray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Great_Tigray), the [Tigray Independence Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigray_Independence_Party), and [Salsay Weyane Tigray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salsay_Weyane_Tigray) describing their eight pre-conditions for peace in February 2021;[^cite_note-409] a mediation group called "A3+1", (consisting of three African countries, [Kenya](188171.md), [Niger](21373.md) and [Tunisia](30188.md), and one non-African country, [Saint Vincent and the Grenadines](27228.md)) in July–August 2021;[^cite_note-410] and a March–August 2022 ceasefire wherein Ethiopian and Tigrayan officials attempted to negotiate a peaceful end to the conflict.[^cite_note-AN_AGF-2022-244]

On 2 November 2022, the Ethiopian government and Tigrayan leaders signed a [peace accord](72171064.md), with the [African Union](10634933.md) as a mediator, and agreed on "orderly, smooth and coordinated disarmament".[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412] The agreement was made effective the next day on 3 November, marking the second anniversary of the war.[^cite_note-Addis_Standard-2022-49]

As part of this process, Prime Minister [Abiy Ahmed](56624029.md) appointed TPLF's [Getachew Reda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getachew_Reda) as head of the [Interim Regional Administration of Tigray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Regional_Administration_of_Tigray), and the Ethiopian parliament removed the TPLF from its terrorism list.[^cite_note-413]

## Reactions

| \[icon] | This section **needs expansion**. You can help by [adding missing information](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Tigray_war\&action=edit\&section=). *(November 2023)* |
| --- | --- |

Main article: [Reactions to the Tigray war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_Tigray_war)

The Tigray war has been the subject of numerous reactions and protests, both locally[^cite_note-414] and worldwide.[^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417]

The [Commission of Inquiry on Tigray Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_of_Inquiry_on_Tigray_Genocide) was established by Tigrayan authorities in May 2022 to document the conflict. A report was released in October 2025, documenting their findings.[^cite_note-418]

## Aftermath

### Health impacts



A wounded child at [Ayder Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayder_Hospital) in Mekelle, Tigray

The war had a devastating impact on the [healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care) system of Tigray; of the 853 health facilities in the region, 86% were at least partially damaged; 232 of them were left "completely unusable", and 28 were destroyed entirely.[^cite_note-419]

It also led to a higher rate of [maternal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_death) and [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) in the Tigray Region. In a study funded by [UNFPA Ethiopia](63952.md) and [UNICEF Ethiopia](19698110.md), it was estimated that maternal mortality rates had increased from 186 deaths per 100,000 people pre-war to 840 deaths per 100,000 people post-war.[^cite_note-420] A health coordinator at the Tigray Health Bureau said to *[Addis Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addis_Standard)* in June 2023, that, according to their research, both maternal and infant mortality rates had regressed to the levels they were at in 2001.[^cite_note-KMG_Aug_2023-421]

According to Tigrayan health official Tsegay Gidey, 81% of mothers in the [Seharti Samre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saharti_Samre) [woreda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woreda) had [birth defects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_defect), and 32 newborn infants had died between January and June 2023.[^cite_note-KMG_Aug_2023-421]

### Economic impact

The war had a costly impact on the Ethiopian economy. In late November 2022, the cost of rebuilding was estimated to be around \$20 billion.[^cite_note-20bil-90][^cite_note-422] The cost of rebuilding healthcare facilities in the Tigray, Afar and Amhara Regions was at least \$1.4 billion, being devastated by the war.[^cite_note-423] Between June 2022 and March 2023, [government debt](695460.md) had increased by 6%, reaching a debt total of roughly \$60.6 billion (3.3 trillion [birr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethiopian_birr)).[^cite_note-Addis_Standard-2023-424]

The Tigray war also damaged the country's relationship with foreign [creditors](601598.md).[^cite_note-Addis_Standard-2023-424] Shortly after it began in November 2020, the [European Union](9317.md) suspended [budget support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_support) to Ethiopia (citing reports of human rights abuses), a decision they would maintain until the end of the war.[^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426] The [credit agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_rating_agency) [Fitch Ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitch_Ratings) downgraded Ethiopia's [credit rating](1194146.md) in January 2023, expressing lower confidence that the Ethiopian government would be able to pay off their [external debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt).[^cite_note-427]

[Inflation](38286.md) hit the country hard in the months following the war. Ethiopia had an average inflation rate of 30% in 2022, largely driven by food prices;[^cite_note-France_24-2022-428] in February 2023, the overall inflation rate reached 32%;[^cite_note-429] by 12 September 2023 ([Enkutatash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enkutatash)), Ethiopia continued to experience high inflation, with commonly purchased food items becoming more expensive.[^cite_note-AN_AP-2023-430] Although the war has not been considered the sole reason for these economic struggles, it has still been described as a contributing factor.[^cite_note-France_24-2022-428][^cite_note-AN_AP-2023-430]

### Continued Eritrean occupation

Although the war largely came to a halt after the peace agreement was signed, Eritrea continues to occupy parts of Tigray as of mid-2023.[^cite_note-\:irob-67] The EDF has been responsible for the deaths of thousands of people in northern Ethiopia since November 2022; from 17 to 25 November alone, Eritrea was reported to have destroyed 241 houses and killed at least 111 people.[^cite_note-431] by 30 December, it was estimated that Eritrean and Amhara forces killed 3,700 since the signing of the peace deal.[^cite_note-York-2023-432]

The Tigray Health Bureau noted that 852 cases of rape and sexual assault were reported between November and December 2022; according to aid workers and interviews with survivors, most of these were committed by Eritrean forces.[^cite_note-433]

As of January 2023, over half of [Irob district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irob_\(Ethiopian_District\)) was occupied by Eritrea.[^cite_note-York-2023-432] Irob advocacy groups and former residents have described it as a "de-facto [annexation](18950961.md)" of the area.[^cite_note-\:irob-67] A religious Irob leader told *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* in August 2023 that Eritrea was blocking off international aid to the area, and lamented that "there has been no improvement for us since the peace."[^cite_note-\:irob-67]

According to a report published in June 2025 by United States based group [The Sentry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sentry_\(organization\)), the EDF have continued this practice of occupation of the Tigray region.[^cite_note-Power_and_Plunder-434] This practice has been aided by the lifting of sanctions unilaterally by the United Nations, which has also created conditions for the EDF and TPLF to fund and train Fano militias.[^cite_note-Power_and_Plunder-434][^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436]

### Fear for new renewed conflict

In late January 2026, [clashes](82270669.md) erupted in the Tigray region. Fighting involved Tigrayan forces and Ethiopian federal troops supported by [Amhara](1746709.md) militias in parts of western Tigray that Amhara forces had not withdrawn from despite the 2022 Pretoria peace agreement. Diplomatic sources confirmed the clashes but said the situation on the ground remained unclear.[^cite_note-437] The TPLF said its central committee "has decided to reinstate the Tigray Government Assembly (parliament), which had been suspended in the name of peace".[^cite_note-438] The statement accused the federal government of violating the 2022 Pretoria Agreement, which ended the war, and of provoking armed conflict within the Tigray region. It also accused the government of withholding funds to pay local civil servants.[^cite_note-439]

[^cite_note-martinplaut-35]: &#x20;Negash, Emnet (24 May 2023). Retrieved 4 July 2023. As our estimate of the civilian deaths in the Tigray war is regularly mentioned in the media, it seems important to share our evolving understanding and updated (lower) number of civilian deaths as a result of the Tigray war and blockade. We concluded that the IPC/FEWS categorization, on which our Tigray statistics are mainly based, overestimates hunger mortality. Along with developing information on the ground, this would point to a total number of civilian deaths ranging from 162,000 to 378,000.

Retrieved 7 April 2021. This conflict has the potential to be devastating. Some scholars have warned Ethiopia could break apart in the way Yugoslavia did in the 1990s. The government has downplayed the fighting, calling it a "law enforcement operation." Kiya Tsegaye, an Ethiopian political analyst, says the government has alienated Tigray from its neighbors. Abiy has made deals with Eritrea and the new government of Sudan, leaving Tigray with few ways to receive the weapons it would need to keep fighting. But he says the Tigray forces are no ordinary militia. "They have dominated the security and the military for almost three decades, and they have all the information and the top secrets of this country," he says. "They know the Achilles' heel." The government alleges that when TPLF fighters attacked their troops last week, they also stole missiles that may be able to reach the capital Addis Ababa.
